People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 67 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I’ve gotten stuck doing these little routines—washing my hands again after handling my wrist brace, rechecking the stove and front door, silently counting through steps before I leave—and they’re starting to make me late even when I know I’ve already done enough.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something dirty, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these routines, like washing your hands or checking the stove, to make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. It’s like I know I’ve already checked, or washed enough, but if I don’t do it again, the thought just sits there. So I recheck the stove or the door, or wash after touching the wrist brace, and it settles me a bit… though then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these routines, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not in a clear way every time. It’s more that I get uneasy that something’s not right, so I wash again after the wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again, or count through things before leaving. I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been getting stuck in these little routines. Washing my hands again after handling my wrist brace, checking the stove and front door more than once… sometimes counting through steps before I leave. It’s not dramatic, but it’s making me late, even when I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or ideas that keep coming into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something dirty, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these routines, like washing your hands or checking the stove, to make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. It’s like I know I’ve already checked, or washed enough, but if I don’t do it again, the thought just sits there. So I recheck the stove or the door, or wash after touching the wrist brace, and it settles me a bit… though then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these routines, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not in a clear way every time. It’s more that I get uneasy that something’s not right, so I wash again after the wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again, or count through things before leaving. I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, specifically intended to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s partly to stop something bad happening, yes. The stove, the door… washing after I’ve handled my wrist brace. But honestly it also just quiets that anxious feeling for a bit. If I don’t do it, or don’t count through things before I leave, I feel stuck there—and then I’m late, even though I know I’ve checked enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive hand-washing actions mostly because you're afraid of coming into contact with germs or contaminated substances?\n\nPatient: Mostly after touching the wrist brace, yes. I don’t know that I’d call it germs exactly, but it feels… unclean, and I end up washing again even when I know I probably don’t need to.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of something being unclean when you touch your wrist brace what makes you want to wash your hands again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s mostly it. When I handle the wrist brace, it feels unclean somehow, and then I get stuck washing my hands again, even if part of me knows I’ve probably done enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been getting stuck in these little routines. Washing my hands again after handling my wrist brace, checking the stove and front door more than once… sometimes counting through steps before I leave. It’s not dramatic, but it’s making me late, even when I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or ideas that keep coming into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something dirty, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these routines, like washing your hands or checking the stove, to make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. It’s like I know I’ve already checked, or washed enough, but if I don’t do it again, the thought just sits there. So I recheck the stove or the door, or wash after touching the wrist brace, and it settles me a bit… though then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these routines, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not in a clear way every time. It’s more that I get uneasy that something’s not right, so I wash again after the wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again, or count through things before leaving. I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, specifically intended to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s partly to stop something bad happening, yes. The stove, the door… washing after I’ve handled my wrist brace. But honestly it also just quiets that anxious feeling for a bit. If I don’t do it, or don’t count through things before I leave, I feel stuck there—and then I’m late, even though I know I’ve checked enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive hand-washing actions mostly because you're afraid of coming into contact with germs or contaminated substances?\n\nPatient: Mostly after touching the wrist brace, yes. I don’t know that I’d call it germs exactly, but it feels… unclean, and I end up washing again even when I know I probably don’t need to.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of something being unclean when you touch your wrist brace what makes you want to wash your hands again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s mostly it. When I handle the wrist brace, it feels unclean somehow, and then I get stuck washing my hands again, even if part of me knows I’ve probably done enough.\n\nDoctor: Is the main reason you wash your hands after touching the wrist brace because you fear it might contaminate you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s the idea. It’s not always a clear fear of germs, exactly… more that I’ve touched something unclean and then there’s this thought that I might carry it around or put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when washing your hands after touching the wrist brace that you might be contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… in a way. It’s more that after I touch it, this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, or that I could somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it’s hard to let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, specifically intended to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s partly to stop something bad happening, yes. The stove, the door… washing after I’ve handled my wrist brace. But honestly it also just quiets that anxious feeling for a bit. If I don’t do it, or don’t count through things before I leave, I feel stuck there—and then I’m late, even though I know I’ve checked enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive hand-washing actions mostly because you're afraid of coming into contact with germs or contaminated substances?\n\nPatient: Mostly after touching the wrist brace, yes. I don’t know that I’d call it germs exactly, but it feels… unclean, and I end up washing again even when I know I probably don’t need to.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of something being unclean when you touch your wrist brace what makes you want to wash your hands again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s mostly it. When I handle the wrist brace, it feels unclean somehow, and then I get stuck washing my hands again, even if part of me knows I’ve probably done enough.\n\nDoctor: Is the main reason you wash your hands after touching the wrist brace because you fear it might contaminate you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s the idea. It’s not always a clear fear of germs, exactly… more that I’ve touched something unclean and then there’s this thought that I might carry it around or put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when washing your hands after touching the wrist brace that you might be contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… in a way. It’s more that after I touch it, this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, or that I could somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it’s hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of causing harm to others more distressing to you than the thought of being contaminated yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s less about me feeling dirty, and more that thought that I might somehow put someone else at risk. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, it sticks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, specifically intended to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s partly to stop something bad happening, yes. The stove, the door… washing after I’ve handled my wrist brace. But honestly it also just quiets that anxious feeling for a bit. If I don’t do it, or don’t count through things before I leave, I feel stuck there—and then I’m late, even though I know I’ve checked enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive hand-washing actions mostly because you're afraid of coming into contact with germs or contaminated substances?\n\nPatient: Mostly after touching the wrist brace, yes. I don’t know that I’d call it germs exactly, but it feels… unclean, and I end up washing again even when I know I probably don’t need to.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of something being unclean when you touch your wrist brace what makes you want to wash your hands again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s mostly it. When I handle the wrist brace, it feels unclean somehow, and then I get stuck washing my hands again, even if part of me knows I’ve probably done enough.\n\nDoctor: Is the main reason you wash your hands after touching the wrist brace because you fear it might contaminate you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s the idea. It’s not always a clear fear of germs, exactly… more that I’ve touched something unclean and then there’s this thought that I might carry it around or put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when washing your hands after touching the wrist brace that you might be contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… in a way. It’s more that after I touch it, this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, or that I could somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it’s hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of causing harm to others more distressing to you than the thought of being contaminated yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s less about me feeling dirty, and more that thought that I might somehow put someone else at risk. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, it sticks.\n\nDoctor: Is the idea of causing harm to others the main thing that's bothering you in these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly that, I think. It’s this thought that I may have left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, and then somehow someone else could be put at risk. I do know it’s out of proportion, but it still barges in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's obsessive thoughts are predominantly about harm to others or themselves", "question": "Is the fear of harming others the main content of your unwanted thoughts, rather than fear of harm to yourself?"}

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been getting stuck in these little routines. Washing my hands again after handling my wrist brace, checking the stove and front door more than once… sometimes counting through steps before I leave. It’s not dramatic, but it’s making me late, even when I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or ideas that keep coming into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something dirty, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these routines, like washing your hands or checking the stove, to make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. It’s like I know I’ve already checked, or washed enough, but if I don’t do it again, the thought just sits there. So I recheck the stove or the door, or wash after touching the wrist brace, and it settles me a bit… though then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these routines, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not in a clear way every time. It’s more that I get uneasy that something’s not right, so I wash again after the wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again, or count through things before leaving. I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, specifically intended to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s partly to stop something bad happening, yes. The stove, the door… washing after I’ve handled my wrist brace. But honestly it also just quiets that anxious feeling for a bit. If I don’t do it, or don’t count through things before I leave, I feel stuck there—and then I’m late, even though I know I’ve checked enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive hand-washing actions mostly because you're afraid of coming into contact with germs or contaminated substances?\n\nPatient: Mostly after touching the wrist brace, yes. I don’t know that I’d call it germs exactly, but it feels… unclean, and I end up washing again even when I know I probably don’t need to.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of something being unclean when you touch your wrist brace what makes you want to wash your hands again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s mostly it. When I handle the wrist brace, it feels unclean somehow, and then I get stuck washing my hands again, even if part of me knows I’ve probably done enough.\n\nDoctor: Is the main reason you wash your hands after touching the wrist brace because you fear it might contaminate you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s the idea. It’s not always a clear fear of germs, exactly… more that I’ve touched something unclean and then there’s this thought that I might carry it around or put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when washing your hands after touching the wrist brace that you might be contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… in a way. It’s more that after I touch it, this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, or that I could somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it’s hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of causing harm to others more distressing to you than the thought of being contaminated yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s less about me feeling dirty, and more that thought that I might somehow put someone else at risk. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, it sticks.\n\nDoctor: Is the idea of causing harm to others the main thing that's bothering you in these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly that, I think. It’s this thought that I may have left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, and then somehow someone else could be put at risk. I do know it’s out of proportion, but it still barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of harming others the main content of your unwanted thoughts, rather than fear of harm to yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say so. It’s not really that I think I’ll be harmed. It’s more this thought that I might’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, and then somehow put someone else at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a specific order or pattern to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I like things fairly tidy, but I don’t think I have to arrange them in a certain pattern to feel okay.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a specific order or pattern to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I like things fairly tidy, but I don’t think I have to arrange them in a certain pattern to feel okay.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your obsessive thoughts more often about causing harm to others or about coming into contact with something contaminated?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 67 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I’ll be reading at home or getting ready for a museum shift, and an ugly little thought keeps barging in that I’ve left something unsafe, touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk, even though I know perfectly well it’s out of proportion.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been getting stuck in these little routines. Washing my hands again after handling my wrist brace, checking the stove and front door more than once… sometimes counting through steps before I leave. It’s not dramatic, but it’s making me late, even when I know I’ve already done enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or ideas that keep coming into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I’ll be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something dirty, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets stuck there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these routines, like washing your hands or checking the stove, to make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose so. It’s like I know I’ve already checked, or washed enough, but if I don’t do it again, the thought just sits there. So I recheck the stove or the door, or wash after touching the wrist brace, and it settles me a bit… though then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these routines, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not in a clear way every time. It’s more that I get uneasy that something’s not right, so I wash again after the wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again, or count through things before leaving. I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, specifically intended to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s partly to stop something bad happening, yes. The stove, the door… washing after I’ve handled my wrist brace. But honestly it also just quiets that anxious feeling for a bit. If I don’t do it, or don’t count through things before I leave, I feel stuck there—and then I’m late, even though I know I’ve checked enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive hand-washing actions mostly because you're afraid of coming into contact with germs or contaminated substances?\n\nPatient: Mostly after touching the wrist brace, yes. I don’t know that I’d call it germs exactly, but it feels… unclean, and I end up washing again even when I know I probably don’t need to.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of something being unclean when you touch your wrist brace what makes you want to wash your hands again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s mostly it. When I handle the wrist brace, it feels unclean somehow, and then I get stuck washing my hands again, even if part of me knows I’ve probably done enough.\n\nDoctor: Is the main reason you wash your hands after touching the wrist brace because you fear it might contaminate you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s the idea. It’s not always a clear fear of germs, exactly… more that I’ve touched something unclean and then there’s this thought that I might carry it around or put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when washing your hands after touching the wrist brace that you might be contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… in a way. It’s more that after I touch it, this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, or that I could somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it’s hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of causing harm to others more distressing to you than the thought of being contaminated yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s less about me feeling dirty, and more that thought that I might somehow put someone else at risk. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, it sticks.\n\nDoctor: Is the idea of causing harm to others the main thing that's bothering you in these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly that, I think. It’s this thought that I may have left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, and then somehow someone else could be put at risk. I do know it’s out of proportion, but it still barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of harming others the main content of your unwanted thoughts, rather than fear of harm to yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say so. It’s not really that I think I’ll be harmed. It’s more this thought that I might’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, and then somehow put someone else at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a specific order or pattern to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I like things fairly tidy, but I don’t think I have to arrange them in a certain pattern to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts more often about causing harm to others or about coming into contact with something contaminated?\n\nPatient: More about causing harm to others, I think. The contamination part comes up too, but usually it’s tied to this worry that I touched something unclean or left something unsafe and then someone else could be put at risk. It’s… unpleasant, even though I know it’s out of proportion.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

So I recheck the stove or the door, or wash after touching the wrist brace, and it settles me a bit… though then I’m late.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something bad from happening when you do these routines, like someone getting hurt?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… not in a clear way every time. It’s more that I get uneasy that something’s not right, so I wash again after the wrist brace, or check the stove and the front door again, or count through things before leaving. I know I’ve already done it, but it feels safer to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these routines are necessary to prevent something bad or harmful from happening to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s the feeling. If I don’t wash again after the wrist brace, or recheck the stove and front door, it feels like I’m leaving something risky undone. I do know I’ve probably done enough, but counting through things before I leave makes it feel… safer, I guess. It’s been making me late.\n\nDoctor: Do you find these recurring thoughts unpleasant or unwelcome when they appear?\n\nPatient: Yes… they’re not thoughts I want. I can be reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this unpleasant idea just barges in — that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s still hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: Are your unwanted thoughts mostly about one kind of thing, like safety or cleanliness?\n\nPatient: Mostly safety and cleanliness, I suppose. It’s like… I’ll be reading or getting ready for the museum, and this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but there it is.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, always triggered by a specific thought or do you sometimes do them just because it feels anxious not to?\n\nPatient: A bit of both, I think. Sometimes there’s a thought behind it, like I might’ve missed something. Other times it’s more just… if I don’t do the washing or checking, or count through things before I leave, I feel unsettled. Even when I know I’ve done enough, it’s hard to leave it alone, and then I end up late.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive actions, like washing your hands or checking the stove, specifically intended to prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about reducing a general feeling of anxiety?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s partly to stop something bad happening, yes. The stove, the door… washing after I’ve handled my wrist brace. But honestly it also just quiets that anxious feeling for a bit. If I don’t do it, or don’t count through things before I leave, I feel stuck there—and then I’m late, even though I know I’ve checked enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repetitive hand-washing actions mostly because you're afraid of coming into contact with germs or contaminated substances?\n\nPatient: Mostly after touching the wrist brace, yes. I don’t know that I’d call it germs exactly, but it feels… unclean, and I end up washing again even when I know I probably don’t need to.\n\nDoctor: Is the feeling of something being unclean when you touch your wrist brace what makes you want to wash your hands again?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s mostly it. When I handle the wrist brace, it feels unclean somehow, and then I get stuck washing my hands again, even if part of me knows I’ve probably done enough.\n\nDoctor: Is the main reason you wash your hands after touching the wrist brace because you fear it might contaminate you or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose that’s the idea. It’s not always a clear fear of germs, exactly… more that I’ve touched something unclean and then there’s this thought that I might carry it around or put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern when washing your hands after touching the wrist brace that you might be contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: Yes… in a way. It’s more that after I touch it, this ugly little thought barges in that I’ve touched something unclean, or that I could somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it’s hard to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is the thought of causing harm to others more distressing to you than the thought of being contaminated yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s less about me feeling dirty, and more that thought that I might somehow put someone else at risk. Even when I know it doesn’t really make sense, it sticks.\n\nDoctor: Is the idea of causing harm to others the main thing that's bothering you in these unwanted thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly that, I think. It’s this thought that I may have left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, and then somehow someone else could be put at risk. I do know it’s out of proportion, but it still barges in.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of harming others the main content of your unwanted thoughts, rather than fear of harm to yourself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’d say so. It’s not really that I think I’ll be harmed. It’s more this thought that I might’ve left something unsafe, or touched something unclean, and then somehow put someone else at risk. I know it’s probably out of proportion, but it still gets in.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a specific order or pattern to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I like things fairly tidy, but I don’t think I have to arrange them in a certain pattern to feel okay.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts more often about causing harm to others or about coming into contact with something contaminated?\n\nPatient: More about causing harm to others, I think. The contamination part comes up too, but usually it’s tied to this worry that I touched something unclean or left something unsafe and then someone else could be put at risk. It’s… unpleasant, even though I know it’s out of proportion.\n\nDoctor: Are your obsessive thoughts more often about causing harm to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly that. It’ll be when I’m just reading at home, or getting ready to go in for a museum shift, and this thought barges in that I’ve left something unsafe or somehow put someone at risk. I know it’s out of proportion, but it’s hard to just dismiss it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F42.1", "F42.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}
